<meta>This page continues the discussion of the three alchemical principles. While the previous page (171) concluded by describing Salt as a "key" that opens "gates," this page transitions into a practical argument: alchemists should work with the "Proximate Matter" (Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt) rather than the "Remote Matter" (the four Elements), as nature has already done the preliminary work of refinement.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>

<header>### ON SULFUR ###</header>

...more extensive details on these matters will be provided in the third treatise on the principles, concerning **Salt**. Now to the point: these **three Principles** are entirely necessary, because they are the **proximate matter** <term>Proximate matter: The "immediate" substance or building blocks nature uses to create something, as opposed to the raw, basic elements.</term>. For the matter of **metals** is twofold: **Proximate** and **Remote**.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The **Proximate** is **Sulfur** and **Mercury**; the **Remote** are the **four Elements** <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>, from which it belongs to God alone to create things. Leave the Elements alone, therefore, because you will achieve nothing with them, nor will you be able to produce anything except these three Principles, since nature itself produces nothing else from them.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;If, therefore, you can produce nothing other than these three principles from the Elements, why undergo the vain labor of seeking or wishing to make that which has already been generated by nature? Is it not better to travel three miles rather than four? <note>original: *Nonne melius est ire tria milliaria quam quator?* This is a common-sense alchemical argument: why start from the absolute beginning (the elements) when nature has already done the first step for you by creating the principles?</note> Let it suffice you, then, to possess the three principles from which nature produces all things within and upon the earth; you will find these three whole in every thing. From the proper separation and conjunction of these, nature produces both **metals** and **stones** in the **mineral kingdom**.

<summary>The author distinguishes between the "Proximate Matter" (Sulfur and Mercury) and the "Remote Matter" (the four elements) of metals, arguing that the alchemist should begin work with the three principles because nature has already evolved them from the basic elements.</summary>

## ON SULFUR

Among the three **Principles** <term>Principles</term> <note>The "Tria Prima" or three primary substances—Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt—which Paracelsian alchemy held to be the building blocks of all matter.</note>, the Philosophers have deservedly placed **Sulfur** in the first position, as it is the most worthy Principle, in whose preparation the entirety of the science lies hidden. For sulfur is threefold, and must be chosen from among others: there is the sulfur that **tinges** <note>original: "tingens"; the power to "stain" or transform the color and nature of a base metal into a noble one.</note> or colors; the sulfur that congeals Mercury; and the third is the **essential ripening sulfur**. 

We truly ought to have treated this matter seriously; but because we have already published one of the principles by way of a **Dialogue** <note>likely referring to the *Dialogue between Mercury, the Alchemist, and Nature*, a famous text often attributed to Michael Sendivogius.</note>, we are likewise compelled to finish the others in the same manner, lest we seem to do an injustice to any of them by changing the style. 

Sulfur is more mature than the other Principles, and Mercury is never **coagulated** <term>coagulated</term> <note>The process of "fixing" a volatile liquid into a solid state; in this context, turning liquid quicksilver into solid gold or silver.</note> except by sulfur. Therefore, our entire operation in this art is nothing else than knowing how to extract sulfur from metals, by which our **quicksilver** <note>original: "argentum viuum"; literally "living silver," the common name for Mercury.</note> is congealed into gold and silver within the bowels of the earth. This sulfur, indeed, serves the role of the **male** <note>In alchemical allegory, Sulfur is the active masculine seed that acts upon the passive feminine Mercury to "generate" metals.</note> in this work,

This man, however, used to say: "If it had happened to me to speak with *Mercury* in that way, I would have fished the truth out of him in a few words; that other fool," he said, "did not know how to proceed with him. If that storm had not disturbed us, we would have concluded that it is the first matter <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "primam materiam." The fundamental, undifferentiated substance from which all metals were thought to originate.</note>; since light or trivial things are not accustomed to be in my head; my head is full of profound imaginings."

And so, confirmed in this opinion, he resolved to work on sulfur. He began, therefore, to distill it, sublimate it, calcine it, roast it, and to make "oil by the bell" <note>original: "oleum per campanam." A process where sulfur is burned under a glass bell-shaped vessel to collect the resulting acidic moisture.</note> from it—doing so both by itself and with crystals and eggshells <note>Eggshells were often used in alchemical recipes as a source of "calx" or calcium.</note>. He attempted many other labors upon it; and when he had consumed much time and expense, and was able to find nothing suited to his purpose, he was sad: wretch-

> "We are wise men; we can even hear the grass grow. If the Art were true, we above all others would have possessed it."

We have even demonstrated this through the work itself several times before men worthy of such a spectacle—men of both high and low social standing. (Though this medicine <note>original: *medicina*; in this context, it refers to the "Tincture" or the "Philosophers' Stone" itself, which was believed to heal both metals and the human body.</note> was not made by our own hands, but we received such things from a friend; nonetheless, they were most genuine). <meta>This page continues from page 216, which concluded a narrative parable about an Alchemist and Saturn. Page 217 begins the "Conclusion" of the treatise on Sulfur, transitioning from allegory to a philosophical summary of alchemical theory.</meta>

<page-num>217</page-num>

-># ON SULFUR<-

<meta>catchword: by imitating (tando)</meta>

## ->CONCLUSION<-

Every seeker of this art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, often called the "Hermetic Art" or "Sacred Art."</note> must first, with mature judgment, examine the creation, operation, and virtues of the **four elements**, along with their actions: for if he is ignorant of their origin and nature, he will not arrive at a knowledge of the **principles** <note>original: "principia"; in alchemy, these are usually Sulfur, Mercury, and Salt, the fundamental components of all matter.</note>, nor will he recognize the true matter of the **stone** <term>stone: *lapis*; the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of the alchemical work.</term>, and much less will he attain a successful end; since every end is bounded by its beginning <note>original: "omnis finis supra suum principium terminatur"; a philosophical maxim suggesting that the result of a process is determined and limited by its starting materials.</note>: He who knows well what he begins, will know well what he will finish. 

For the origin of the elements is **chaos** <note>original: "chaos"; the primal, unformed state of matter from which God shaped the universe.</note>, out of which **God the creator** of things created and separated the elements, a task which belongs to God alone. From the elements, truly, **nature** produces the principles of things, and this—through the will of God—belongs to nature alone. From these principles, nature afterwards produces minerals and all things: from which the **craftsman** <term>craftsman: *artifex*; the alchemist who works as a "minister of nature" to accelerate or perfect natural processes.</term> also, by imitating <note>The sentence ends here and continues on the next page with the word "imitando" (imitating).</note>

<summary>This page concludes the discourse on sulfur by emphasizing that an alchemist must understand the four elements and the divine origin of matter to succeed. It defines a hierarchy of creation moving from God (chaos to elements) to Nature (elements to principles) to the Alchemist (imitating nature).</summary>

<keywords>four elements, chaos, God the creator, Philosopher's stone, principles of nature, alchemist as craftsman, sulfur treatise</keywords>

--- Page 240 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Conclusion" of the treatise on Sulfur from page 217. It transitions from the theological origins of the elements to the practical hierarchy of matter—from elements to principles (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury) to metals—emphasizing that the alchemist must follow the same intermediate steps as nature.</meta>

<page-num>218</page-num>

-># ON SULFUR<-

<meta>catchword: coagulant (coagulum)</meta>

by imitating nature, he can perform many wonders.

<margin><image-desc>a hand with a pointing finger</image-desc></margin> Since nature produces minerals, metals, and all kinds of things from these **principles**—which are **Salt**, **Sulfur**, and **Mercury**—and does not produce a metal directly from the **elements**, but rather through these principles which act as a medium between the elements and the metals: Therefore, if nature itself does not work that way, much less can **art** <note>original: "ars"; refers to alchemy, the "Art" that assists or accelerates natural processes.</note> succeed. And it is not only in this example, but in every natural process, that the intermediate arrangement must be observed. 

For this reason, in this treatise, we have described both the elements themselves and their actions and operations, as well as the origin of the principles, quite extensively (providing clearer explanations than any of the **Philosophers** <note>original: "Philosophorum"; the traditional authorities on alchemy and natural philosophy.</note> have given until now). This was done so that a dedicated seeker might more easily understand the degree to which the **stone** <term>stone: *lapis*; the Philosopher's Stone, the ultimate goal of the work.</term> differs from metals, and how metals differ from the elements. 

Indeed, there is a difference between **gold** and **water**, but a smaller difference exists between water and **Mercury**, and the smallest difference of all is between gold and Mercury. For the "house" <note>In alchemical theory, a "house" is a substance that contains, protects, or serves as the base for another; here, gold is seen as being "rooted" in Mercury.</note> of gold is Mercury, and the house of Mercury is water; but **sulfur** is the...

<summary>This page outlines the alchemical hierarchy of matter, explaining that nature moves from elements to the "Three Principles" (Salt, Sulfur, Mercury) before forming metals. It asserts that the alchemist must follow this natural sequence, identifying Mercury as the "house" or foundational substance of gold.</summary>

# ON SULFUR

<meta>catchword: ta (refers to 'tamen' or similar beginning next section)</meta>

the coagulant of **Mercury**; which sulfur is indeed most difficult to prepare, but even more difficult to investigate. <margin><image-desc>A drawing of a manicule (pointing hand) in the right margin points towards the text discussing the hidden secrets of the philosophers' sulfur.</image-desc></margin> Since in the **Sulfur of the Philosophers** all this secret <term>secret: *arcanum*; a profound mystery or hidden power of nature.</term> lies hidden, which is also contained in the innermost parts of **Mercury**. Regarding its preparation—without which it is useless—we shall eventually provide instruction in the third of the principles, concerning **Salt**, since in this place we are treating the virtue and origin of **Sulfur**, and not its practice <note>original: "praxim"; refers to the manual laboratory work or "recipe" as opposed to the theory.</note>.

Yet we have not written this treatise here for the purpose of wanting to refute the ancient **Philosophers**, but rather to confirm their writings and to fulfill those things which they omitted. For since the Philosophers are also human beings, they cannot perform all things with such perfect precision, nor can any single person suffice for all things. Some have even been seduced away from the linear path of nature by miracles, as we read happened to **Albertus Magnus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a Dominican friar and bishop who became famous for his works on natural philosophy and was often legendary in alchemical circles.</note>, a most wise man and Philosopher. He wrote that in his time, grains of gold <note>original: "grana aurea"</note> were found between the teeth of a dead man in a tomb...

<summary>This page defines Sulfur as the necessary solidifying agent for Mercury and notes that the secret of the "Philosophers' Sulfur" is difficult to uncover. The author also defends his work as a completion of the ancient masters' knowledge, citing Albertus Magnus as a great thinker who nonetheless encountered natural anomalies.</summary>

...that it was so. He could not find a certain reason for this miracle, but attributed it to a mineral power within man; and upon this opinion, he felt confirmed by the saying of **Morienus** <note>Morienus was a legendary 7th-century alchemist said to have taught the Umayyad prince Khalid ibn Yazid; his works were central to the medieval Latin alchemical tradition.</note>, who said: *“and this matter, O King, is extracted from you.”* <note>original: "et hac materia, Rex, à te extrahitur." This famous alchemical riddle led many to believe the "Prime Matter" was literally part of the human body (like blood or hair).</note> But this is erroneous, for **Morienus** intended those things to be understood philosophically.

For the mineral virtue <term>virtue: *virtus*; the inherent power, active property, or "life force" of a substance.</term> is located in its own kingdom <term>kingdom: *regnum*; the traditional division of nature into Animal, Vegetable, and Mineral categories.</term>, just as the animal virtue is in its own kingdom, as we have distinguished those kingdoms in the book of the **Twelve Treatises** <note>This likely refers to the *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (New Chemical Light), a famous work often attributed to Michael Sendivogius, which contains twelve treatises on the principles of nature.</note>, and divided them into three kingdoms; because any one of these stands and multiplies within itself without any external entry. It is indeed true that in the animal kingdom there is both **Mercury** as the matter and **Sulfur** as the virtue, but it is animal and not mineral. If the power of animal sulfur were not in man, it would not congeal the blood—which is the animal **Mercury**—into flesh and bones. So also, if the power of vegetable sulfur were not in the vegetable kingdom, it would not congeal water (or "vegetable **Mercury**") into herbs and trees. Thus also in the mineral kingdom it must be underst-

<summary>This page refutes the literal interpretation that the Philosopher's Stone is derived from the human body, clarifying that while all three kingdoms of nature (animal, vegetable, mineral) operate via Mercury and Sulfur, these principles remain specific to their own domains.</summary>

# ON SULFUR

...must be noted. Indeed, these three **Mercuries** do not differ in their virtue <term>virtue: *virtus*; the inherent active power or "life force" of a chemical substance.</term>, nor do those three **Sulfurs**, because any Sulfur has the natural power to coagulate its own Mercury, and any Mercury has the power to be coagulated by its own Sulfur; but not by a foreign one.

As for the reason why **gold** was found or generated between the teeth of a deceased man, the explanation is this: during the life of the deceased, Mercury was introduced into that sick body by some Physician, either through an ointment <note>Mercurial ointments were a standard, though highly toxic, treatment for various ailments, particularly skin diseases and syphilis.</note>, or through **turbith** <term>turbith: *turpethum*; a medicinal root used as a purgative, sometimes combined with mineral treatments.</term>, or by another method, as is the custom and use. It is the nature of Mercury to ascend to the mouth of the patient and to be evacuated through sores in the mouth along with phlegm <note>The author is describing "salivation" or ptyalism, a symptom of mercury poisoning that historical doctors often intentionally induced, believing the "poison" of a disease was being purged through the saliva.</note>.

If, therefore, the sick person died during such a treatment, that Mercury, having no way to exit, remained in the mouth between the teeth. The corpse then became a natural vessel <term>vessel: *vas*; the container, like a flask or crucible, in which alchemical transformation occurs.</term> for the Mercury. Thus, having been shut in for a long time, it was congealed into gold by its own Sulfur, having been purified from the corrosive phlegm of the human body by the natural heat of **putrefaction** <term>putrefaction: *putrefactio*; the process of decay, which alchemists viewed as a vital stage of "cleansing" and "ripening" matter into a higher state.</term>. But if Mercury...

<summary>This page explains that gold found in human corpses is not a biological miracle, but a result of medicinal mercury being "ripened" into gold by the heat of the body's decay. It reinforces the alchemical principle that Mercury can only be "fixed" or coagulated by its own specific Sulfur.</summary>

...mineral **Mercury** had not been introduced, gold would never have been produced. This serves as a most true example of how nature, in the bowels of the earth, produces **gold** and **silver**—and other metals—from **Mercury** alone, according to the arrangement of the location or the **matrix** <term>matrix: *matrix*; the subterranean "womb" or geological cavity where minerals are thought to grow and mature.</term>. For **Mercury** contains its own **Sulfur** within itself, by which it is coagulated <note>original: *coagulatur*; the alchemical process of a liquid becoming solid or "fixed" through heat or chemical reaction.</note> into gold, unless it is hindered by some accident, lacks the required heat, or is not in a sealed place. 

Therefore, it is not the power of "animal sulfur" <note>The author is refuting the idea that human flesh or biological "Sulfur" has the power to create gold; he insists the transformation was purely mineral.</note> that congeals **Mercury** into gold, but rather into flesh; for if such a power existed in man, it would happen in all bodies, which is not the case. Many such miracles and accidental occurrences happen which, if not carefully considered by writers, lead readers into error. A good investigator <term>investigator: *inquisitor*; a researcher or student of natural philosophy who seeks the truth of nature's laws.</term>, however, ought to weigh everything against the possibilities of nature: if they do not align with nature, they are to be disregarded.

It is sufficient for the diligent student to have received the origin of the **Principles** <note>original: *principiorum*; the fundamental components of matter in alchemy, typically Mercury, Sulfur, and Salt.</note> here, since...

<page-num>223</page-num>
<header>ON SULFUR</header>

once the **principle** is known, the end is always in doubt. Concerning these matters, we have addressed the investigator <term>investigator: *inquisitor*; a seeker or student of the secrets of nature.</term> in this treatise not enigmatically <note>original: *aenigmatice*; a reference to the "Hermetic" style of writing where alchemical secrets were hidden behind riddles and metaphors. The author claims to be breaking this tradition of secrecy.</note>, but in the clearest speech we could manage and as much as is permitted to us. 

Through this work, if God illuminates the mind of anyone, he will know what a successor owes to his predecessors, since this science is always acquired through such talents. After this clear demonstration, we place this knowledge back into the care <note>original: *sinum*; literally "the bosom," implying a place of safety and divine protection.</note> of God Most High, our creator and Lord, and we commend ourselves, together with our good readers, into His grace and immense mercy: To Whom be praise and glory through infinite ages of ages.
